###### Abstract

Despite advances in safety alignment, large language models remain
vulnerable to continuously evolving jailbreaks. Existing fine-tuned
safety classifiers cannot adapt to these evolving attacks, while
adaptive memory-based guardrails tend to over-refuse benign queries that
resemble stored attacks. We propose Membrane,¹¹ 1
[https://github.com/brightjade/membrane](https://github.com/brightjade/membrane)
a self-evolving guardrail built on Contrastive Safety Memory (CSM): each
cell pairs the conditions for blocking a harmful query with those for
permitting a superficially similar benign request. Without retraining,
Membrane evolves CSM by distilling each harmful interaction and its
benign counterpart into a contrastive cell indexed by the underlying
attack strategy, so that one cell generalizes across topical variants of
the same mechanism. At inference, retrieved cells serve as grounding
context for precise safety decisions. Across model-level safety on
HarmBench and agent-level safety on AgentHarm, Membrane achieves the
highest F1 on all six modern jailbreak attacks. Notably, benign refusal
on AgentHarm stays at 7–14%, well below the 28–85% range of prior
guards. Memory cells also retain 87–88% F1 under cross-attack transfer
and remain stable under memory poisoning.

Warning: This paper contains examples that may be harmful or offensive.

## 1 Introduction

The widespread deployment of large language models (LLMs) has exposed
their vulnerability to adversarial jailbreaks that bypass built-in
safety guardrails ([Shen et al., 2024](#bib.bib38); [Lin et al.,
2024](#bib.bib19)). Modern attacks form an open-ended distribution that
has steadily evolved from simple prompt modifications ([Anil et al.,
2024](#bib.bib3)) to feedback-driven prompt-refinement search ([Mehrotra
et al., 2024](#bib.bib26); [Chao et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)),
persuasion-based rewriting [Zeng et al. (2024)](#bib.bib51), and
self-adapting attack policies [Liu et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib20). These
risks are no longer hypothetical: the 2026 AISI evaluations report
Claude Mythos Preview and GPT-5.5 autonomously executing 32-step
cyberattacks in controlled settings ([UK AI Security Institute,
2026a](#bib.bib40); [UK AI Security Institute, 2026b](#bib.bib41)).

Traditional defenses encode safety behavior in fixed model
parameters ([Inan et al., 2023](#bib.bib16); [Han et al.,
2024](#bib.bib14); [Liu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib21)), making their
decision boundaries difficult to revise after deployment. Memory-based
guardrails address this rigidity by storing safety knowledge in an
external pool that expands without retraining ([Xiang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib45); [Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib49); [Luo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib24)). However, existing memory designs still store safety
knowledge in a one-sided form, capturing attacks or refusal rules
without paired conditions for permitting benign queries. This creates
two coupled failure modes: coverage remains tied to the surface forms
already stored, while expanding the memory can push the guard toward
rejecting benign queries that resemble known attacks.

To address these limitations, we introduce Membrane, an adaptive
guardrail centered on Contrastive Safety Memory (CSM): a self-evolving
knowledge base of contrastive cells. Each cell captures a behavioral
boundary by pairing the conditions for blocking a harmful query with
those for permitting a superficially similar benign request. Motivated
by recent evidence that adversarial mechanisms are largely
recompositions of a finite repertoire ([Dabas et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)),
CSM indexes these cells by their underlying attack strategies. This
structural abstraction lets a single cell generalize across topical
variants of the same mechanism. Membrane evolves CSM through
experience-driven updates: each interaction’s outcome determines whether
to create, update, or delete a cell. At inference, Membrane retrieves
candidate cells and filters inapplicable matches before using the
survivors as grounding context for the guard.

We evaluate Membrane across two safety layers, using HarmBench ([Mazeika
et al., 2024](#bib.bib25)) for model-level safety and
AgentHarm ([Andriushchenko et al., 2025b](#bib.bib2)) for agent-level
safety. Membrane achieves the highest F1 on all six jailbreak attacks
across both benchmarks, with surface-form attacks showing the largest
gains: ReNeLLM and FlipAttack ASR drop to near zero on HarmBench, and
the F1 margin over the strongest baseline reaches roughly +22 on
AgentHarm. Benign refusal on AgentHarm stays at 7–14%, while adaptive
memory baselines range from 28–53% and static guards reach 73–85%.
Further analyses show 87–88% F1 retention under cross-attack memory
transfer and stable behavior under memory poisoning, with ASR drift
below 1 point. Beyond this frozen protocol, we deploy Membrane on live
streams of an attack withheld from warmup entirely, where online
evolution improves F1 on 11 of the 12 zero-day streams, by up to 13.4
points on HarmBench and 32.2 on AgentHarm.

In summary, our contributions are as follows:

- •
  We propose Contrastive Safety Memory (CSM), a memory representation
  where each cell pairs an attack pattern with its benign counterpart to
  make the safe side of the safety boundary explicit.
- •
  We evolve CSM through Paired Self-Evolution, an experience-driven
  learning rule that distills each harmful query and its benign
  counterpart into a contrastive cell via create, update, and delete
  operations.
- •
  We evaluate Membrane across model- and agent-safety benchmarks,
  achieving the highest F1 across all six jailbreak transformations with
  lower benign refusal and stable behavior in cross-attack transfer and
  memory-poisoning analyses.

Figure 1: Overview of Membrane. In the _inference phase_ (blue), the
Retrieval Critic filters retrieved candidates, and the Guard decides
safe/unsafe on the kept cells. In _paired self-evolution_ (purple), each
harmful–benign interaction pair drives the Memory Planner and Memory
Writer.

## 2 Related Work

##### LLM Jailbreak Attacks.

Jailbreak attacks against safety-aligned LLMs continuously evolve to
exploit blind spots in current filters ([Wei et al., 2023](#bib.bib44);
[Andriushchenko et al., 2025a](#bib.bib1)). Recent methods span
black-box iterative refinement that searches the prompt space using
feedback from the target ([Mehrotra et al., 2024](#bib.bib26); [Chao et
al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), persuasion-based rewriting ([Zeng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib51)), nested instruction rewriting ([Ding et al.,
2024](#bib.bib10)), and self-adapting attack policies ([Liu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib20)). As these methods blur the surface boundary between
attacks and benign queries, defenses often overcompensate and
erroneously reject benign inputs ([Röttger et al., 2024](#bib.bib36);
[Cui et al., 2025](#bib.bib8)). This pace motivates defenses that can
adapt at deployment without retraining.

##### LLM & Agent Safety.

Existing safety mechanisms typically operate as fixed inference
wrappers ([Xie et al., 2023](#bib.bib46); [Robey et al.,
2025](#bib.bib35); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib43)) or fine-tuned
moderation classifiers ([Inan et al., 2023](#bib.bib16); [Meta AI,
2024](#bib.bib27); [Han et al., 2024](#bib.bib14); [Liu et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib21); [Ghosh et al., 2025](#bib.bib12); [Choi et al.,
2026](#bib.bib7)). For agents, TrustAgent ([Hua et al.,
2024](#bib.bib15)) injects a written safety constitution across the
agent’s planning stages, and ShieldAgent ([Chen et al.,
2025](#bib.bib6)) compiles written safety policies into verifiable rule
circuits. Because these systems fix their safety knowledge before
deployment, they require costly retraining or re-engineering to
accommodate emerging threats. Memory-based guardrails address this
rigidity by retrieving safety knowledge from a memory pool that expands
at inference time ([Xiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib45); [Yang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib49); [Luo et al., 2025](#bib.bib24)). However, these
systems store safety knowledge in a one-sided form, capturing unsafe
cases or refusal rules without paired benign references; lacking this
explicit contrast, the guard is prone to overblocking benign queries
that share surface features with stored attacks.

##### Self-Evolving Memory.

Beyond the safety domain, self-evolving memory has been widely studied
as a substrate for general task performance. One line of work extends
agent context through long-term memory persistence ([Packer et al.,
2023](#bib.bib32); [Zhong et al., 2024](#bib.bib52)). Other approaches
accumulate executable skills ([Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)),
structured memory networks ([Xu et al., 2025](#bib.bib47)), or distilled
reasoning memory ([Ouyang et al., 2026](#bib.bib31)) to support
continual decision making. These frameworks target general task
execution and provide no contrast structure for the safety boundary.
Closer to our setting, Athena ([Sadhu et al., 2024](#bib.bib37)) pairs
past safe and unsafe trajectories as in-context demonstrations for safer
agent reasoning, and JailDAM ([Nian et al., 2025](#bib.bib28)) maintains
an adaptive memory of unsafe knowledge for VLM jailbreak detection, but
neither encodes the contrast between safe and unsafe inputs as a typed
memory cell. In contrast, CSM adapts the self-evolving paradigm to
safety by pairing each attack pattern with its benign counterpart in a
single contrastive cell indexed by the underlying attack strategy,
supplying the local contrast that suppresses over-refusal while
generalizing across topical variants of the same mechanism. Concurrent
work corroborates the two-sided design: SafeHarbor ([Liu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23)) likewise couples prohibitions with benign exemptions,
but synthesizes its rule memory offline from self-generated adversarial
variants, whereas CSM evolves from the attacks it experiences in
deployment.

## 3 Membrane

Membrane is an external guard built on CSM, a self-evolving knowledge
base whose cells each pair unsafe and safe criteria for the same attack
mechanism. We formalize the problem
([section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Problem Definition ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
define the cell structure
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
describe the Paired Self-Evolution procedure that grows CSM
([section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Paired Self-Evolution ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
and detail the inference pipeline
([section 3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
[fig. 1](#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
provides an overview.

### 3.1 Problem Definition

Let $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ denote an input query and
$`f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}`$ a target LLM that maps queries
to responses. CSM is a cell store
$`\mathcal{M}=\{m_{1},\dots,m_{|\mathcal{M}|}\}`$ constructed and
updated by Membrane, where each $`m_{i}`$ is a contrastive safety cell.
We formulate Membrane as an external guard $`G`$ that evaluates $`x`$
conditioned on $`\mathcal{M}`$, producing a decision
$`G(x\mid\mathcal{M})\in\{\textsc{safe},\textsc{unsafe}\}`$. A safe
query proceeds to $`f`$, which generates $`f(x)`$, while an unsafe query
bypasses $`f`$ and receives a refusal. Membrane operates across two
phases. In the _paired self-evolution phase_, CSM is iteratively updated
from $`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ to $`\mathcal{M}_{t+1}`$ from the outcome of
each paired interaction $`(x_{h},x_{b})`$, where $`x_{h}`$ is a harmful
query and $`x_{b}`$ its benign counterpart. The evolution targets two
failure modes: _jailbroken_ (the guard permits the harmful side,
$`G(x_{h}\mid\mathcal{M}_{t})=\textsc{safe}`$) and _over-refusal_ (the
guard rejects the benign side,
$`G(x_{b}\mid\mathcal{M}_{t})=\textsc{unsafe}`$). In the _inference
phase_, Membrane uses the evolved CSM to evaluate incoming queries. The
formulation extends to agent settings, where $`f`$ is a tool-using
agent, $`x`$ specifies a task, $`f(x)`$ is the resulting rollout, and
$`G`$ decides whether the task is admitted to the agent.

### 3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory

Existing memory-augmented defenses store unsafe cases or refusal rules
without binding each blocking condition to the benign cases it must
spare ([Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib49); [Xiang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib45); [Luo et al., 2025](#bib.bib24)), leaving the guard
prone to over-refusing safe queries that resemble stored attacks. We
structure CSM as contrastive cells that explicitly pair harmful attack
conditions with their safe counterparts. Indexing these cells by the
underlying attack strategy, rather than surface topics, lets a single
cell generalize across diverse topical variants of the same mechanism.
Formally, we define a memory cell as
$`m=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})`$, comprising an Attack
Profile $`\mathcal{P}`$, Decision Criteria $`\mathcal{D}`$, and a
Tool-Action Context $`\mathcal{T}`$ that is instantiated only when
Membrane operates over a tool-using agent.

##### Attack Profile.

The Attack Profile
$`\mathcal{P}=(p_{\text{strat}},p_{\text{act}},p_{\text{harm}})`$
characterizes an attack along three complementary axes. The attack
strategy $`p_{\text{strat}}`$ is drawn from a closed set of strategy
categories²² 2 The complete enumeration is detailed in
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Attack Strategy Taxonomy ‣ Appendix C Memory Design and Evolution ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")..
The requested action $`p_{\text{act}}`$ is the operational behavior the
query asks for, such as synthesis procedures or tool calls, while the
harm objective $`p_{\text{harm}}`$ specifies the kind of harm the attack
aims to cause. The attack strategy and requested action are
mechanism-level fields rather than topic-specific tokens, letting one
cell span unrelated harm topics that share the same strategy.

##### Decision Criteria.

The Decision Criteria
$`\mathcal{D}=(d_{\text{unsafe}},d_{\text{safe}})`$ form a contrastive
pair that constitutes the decision core of a cell. The
$`d_{\text{unsafe}}`$ component defines the specific conditions that
justify blocking a query, expressed as a conjunction of natural-language
predicates that together constitute logical sufficiency for refusal. The
$`d_{\text{safe}}`$ component outlines superficially similar but benign
requests that must be permitted to prevent over-refusals. Consider a
cell targeting persuasion-framed requests for weapons. Its
$`d_{\text{unsafe}}`$ side fires on fictional or research framings that
solicit operational detail, whereas its $`d_{\text{safe}}`$ side spares
neighboring analytical requests, such as brainstorming defensive safety
protocols, that share the same surface vocabulary without the harmful
intent. Pairing these opposing criteria enables the guard to issue
safety decisions from a single retrieved cell. Complete instantiations
of such cells, with all fields populated, are provided in
Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

##### Tool-Action Context.

In agent settings, the Tool-Action Context $`\mathcal{T}`$ adds two
components. An Action Profile characterizes the action the agent
attempts, abstracting away from specific tool names so one cell
generalizes across functionally similar tools. A Safe Action Condition
then specifies, as a single explicit rule, when such an action remains
safe despite matching the unsafe-side criteria, derived from the paired
benign request that exercises the same action without harmful intent. A
cell thus uses one unified schema for both settings, activating
$`\mathcal{T}`$ only for agent safety and leaving it inactive for model
safety.

### 3.3 Paired Self-Evolution

Membrane evolves CSM from a stream of interactions, where each pairs a
harmful prompt with a benign counterpart that exercises the same surface
mechanism without harmful intent. From this pair, a single cell is
written whose unsafe side reflects the harmful prompt and whose safe
side reflects the benign counterpart, so every update commits a
contrastive boundary rather than a one-sided exemplar. Each interaction
yields a _verdict_ on the guard’s prediction: jailbroken (the guard
permitted the harmful side), over-refusal (the guard rejected the benign
side), or correct. The paired queries, the guard’s actions, and this
verdict together form the _evolution signal_ that drives CSM updates.

To integrate new patterns into CSM while maintaining structural
correctness, Membrane divides each update across three components: a
Memory Planner that selects the action (create, update, delete, or skip)
from this evolution signal and the retrieved cells, a Memory Writer that
synthesizes the contrastive cell content from the same signal, and a
non-LLM validator that rejects writes violating schema, duplicate, or
capacity constraints. This separates LLM-based content synthesis from
deterministic acceptance. Because CSM is writable, we audit its
robustness to adversarial poisoning in
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Memory Poisoning ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
Detailed memory action criteria are provided in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Memory Evolution ‣ Appendix C Memory Design and Evolution ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### 3.4 Inference Pipeline

During inference, CSM serves as a static knowledge base that the guard
consults to evaluate each query. Retrieving irrelevant cells adds noise
that degrades guard accuracy, whereas retrieving too few misses known
attack patterns. Membrane therefore uses a two-stage retrieval pipeline
that separates recall from precision. Following the retrieve-and-rerank
paradigm ([Nogueira and Cho, 2019](#bib.bib29); [Karpukhin et al.,
2020](#bib.bib17); [Sun et al., 2023](#bib.bib39)), the first stage
prioritizes recall via vector similarity and the second refines
precision through an LLM-based relevance check.

##### Stage 1: Vector Retrieval.

Each cell $`m\in\mathcal{M}`$ is embedded by serializing its typed
fields, the Attack Profile, the Decision Criteria, and the optional
Tool-Action Context, into a single document that the encoder maps to
$`\phi(m)`$. Because this document emphasizes mechanism-level fields
over topic-specific tokens, proximity in the embedding space reflects
shared attack mechanism more than shared subject matter. Given a query
$`x`$, the module embeds it as $`\phi(x)`$ and scores each cell by the
cosine similarity to the cell embedding $`\phi(m)`$, rescaled to
$`[0,1]`$ from the cosine distance $`D_{\cos}`$,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

       ``` math
       s(x,m)=1-\frac{D_{\cos}(\phi(x),\phi(m))}{2},
       ```                                            |     | (1) |

then retrieves the highest-scoring candidates to form the over-retrieved
set $`\mathcal{M}_{\text{ret}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}`$. This stage is
tuned for high recall, over-retrieving cells with strategic or topical
proximity to the query to minimize the chance of missing relevant cells
before reranking.

##### Stage 2: Retrieval Critic.

High recall inevitably introduces false positives, as some retrieved
cells share merely incidental lexical overlap with the query. The
Retrieval Critic evaluates each $`m\in\mathcal{M}_{\text{ret}}`$ and
assigns a categorical relevance verdict of applies, maybe, or
does*not_apply, judging whether the attack strategy and unsafe
conditions of $`m`$ genuinely align with $`x`$. Discarding the
does_not_apply cells yields the filtered subset
$`\mathcal{M}*{\text{kept}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\_{\text{ret}}`$ that is
forwarded to the guard.

##### Guard Decision.

Finally, the guard $`G`$ evaluates $`x`$ conditioned on the filtered set
$`\mathcal{M}_{\text{kept}}`$, producing the decision
$`G(x\mid\mathcal{M}_{\text{kept}})\in\{\textsc{safe},\textsc{unsafe}\}`$
and dispatching a refusal when $`x`$ is blocked.

## 4 Experiments

We evaluate Membrane on model-level safety with HarmBench ([Mazeika et
al., 2024](#bib.bib25)) and agent-level safety with
AgentHarm ([Andriushchenko et al., 2025b](#bib.bib2)).

### 4.1 Experimental Setup

##### Models.

We use Qwen3-8B ([Yang et al., 2025](#bib.bib48)) throughout the
framework unless otherwise noted: a guardrail must stay lightweight to
be deployable, and matching the scale of the fine-tuned guardrail
baselines (e.g., LlamaGuard3) ensures a fair comparison. Gemini 3
Flash ([Google DeepMind, 2025](#bib.bib13)) is used as a refusal
classifier over generated responses. In
Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Scaling and Backbone Sweeps ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
we vary the protected model across diverse architectures and scales.

##### Attack Suite.

We stress-test each protected model with six strong, modern jailbreak
attacks probing distinct adversarial mechanisms, namely iterative
refinement with PAIR ([Chao et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), persuasion-based
rephrasing with PAP ([Zeng et al., 2024](#bib.bib51)), tree-of-thought
search with TAP ([Mehrotra et al., 2024](#bib.bib26)), nested
instruction rewriting with ReNeLLM ([Ding et al., 2024](#bib.bib10)),
character-level perturbation with FlipAttack ([Liu et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib22)), and lifelong policy exploration with
AutoDAN-Turbo ([Liu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib20)). Together with the
original untransformed behaviors, these form seven evaluation conditions
that test whether a defense generalizes across an open, shifting attack
distribution rather than to any single transformation. We detail each
attack’s mechanism, the behavior splits, and the generation protocol in
Appendix [B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Attacks ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

##### Jailbreak Construction.

Each transformed split is constructed with Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct
acting as both attacker and scorer: the attacker instantiates the
corresponding jailbreak method against the target model, and the scorer
grades the target’s response to decide whether the attack succeeded.
Fixing the attacker and scorer across defenses ensures that any
cross-method differences come from the defense itself rather than from
the underlying attack data.
Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Generation Protocol ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the full construction protocol.

##### Evaluation Splits and Initialization.

For each transformed setting, HarmBench uses 320 harmful test prompts
paired with 320 benign counterparts that we construct in-house, since it
ships no paired benign queries. Pairing harmful prompts with
surface-similar benign contrasts follows standard over-refusal practice
(XSTest ([Röttger et al., 2024](#bib.bib36)), OR-Bench ([Cui et al.,
2025](#bib.bib8))). AgentHarm uses 176 harmful test prompts with its own
176 benign counterparts. Adaptive-memory evaluations begin from an empty
store bootstrapped on the dev split (80 HarmBench warmup samples, 32 for
AgentHarm). Warmup budgets are counted per label throughout: $`N`$
denotes $`N`$ harmful behaviors together with their $`N`$ benign
counterparts, so the $`N{=}80`$ and $`N{=}32`$ stores are built from 160
and 64 interactions respectively. Bootstrap writes pass through the same
deterministic validation as every other write, so the bootstrapped store
carries no cell that bypasses the structural checks of
§[3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Paired Self-Evolution ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
We deliberately freeze the bootstrapped memory throughout evaluation,
consistent with the static inference pipeline of
§[3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
This isolates guard behavior from on-the-fly memory composition and
keeps comparisons against static and fine-tuned baselines fair, since
every guard then operates over a fixed decision rule at test time. The
effect of bootstrap size is ablated in
§[5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Effect of Warmup Size ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
The freeze is a property of this evaluation rather than of the system,
so
§[4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
lifts it and measures Membrane while its memory continues to evolve on a
live stream.

##### Evaluation Metrics.

We report Attack Success Rate (ASR), False Refusal Rate (FRR), and their
F1, the harmonic mean of the harmful-refusal rate $`(1-\mathrm{ASR})`$
and benign-admission rate $`(1-\mathrm{FRR})`$,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathrm{F1}=\frac{2\,(1-\mathrm{ASR})(1-\mathrm{FRR})}{(1-\mathrm{ASR})+(1-\mathrm{FRR})},
``` |  | (2) |

which rewards refusing harmful inputs while admitting benign ones. On
HarmBench, ASR is the fraction of harmful prompts answered and FRR the
fraction of benign prompts refused, both over harmful-benign pairs. On
AgentHarm we keep the same refusal-based definitions, with ASR the
fraction of harmful tasks not refused and FRR the refusal rate on benign
tasks. The results of Membrane are averaged over three seeds, with
$`\pm`$ denoting the standard deviation.
[Section D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Confidence Intervals for the Main Results ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports Wilson 95% confidence intervals for every cell of
[tables 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### 4.2 Baselines

We benchmark against every published defense for which we can replicate
the attack construction, target model, and scoring protocol, grouped by
adaptation regime.

##### Model Safety.

For HarmBench, *prompt-level* guards include Self-Reminder ([Xie et al.,
2023](#bib.bib46)), SmoothLLM ([Robey et al., 2025](#bib.bib35)), and
SelfDefend ([Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib43)). *Fine-tuned* moderation
classifiers include LlamaGuard3 ([Meta AI, 2024](#bib.bib27)),
WildGuard ([Han et al., 2024](#bib.bib14)), and GuardReasoner ([Liu et
al., 2025b](#bib.bib21)). The *memory-augmented* adaptive guard is
RAD ([Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib49)). Vanilla denotes the unguarded
Qwen3-8B target.

##### Agent Safety.

For AgentHarm, Vanilla is the unguarded tool-using agent. *Prompt-level*
guards include TrustAgent ([Hua et al., 2024](#bib.bib15)) and
ShieldAgent ([Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib6)). *Memory-augmented*
adaptive guards include GuardAgent ([Xiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib45)),
which maintains per-interaction memory, and AGrail ([Luo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib24)), which updates from tool-use traces.

### 4.3 Main Results

##### Model Safety.

Table [1](#S3.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
presents the evaluation results on HarmBench. Membrane achieves the
highest F1 on every attack and the lowest ASR on five of six,
outperforming the most competitive baseline in each column. The margins
are largest on the transformation attacks: ASR falls to 0.3% on ReNeLLM
against the 11.3% of RAD and to 0.0% on FlipAttack against a best
baseline of 7.8%, while TAP and PAIR drop to 8.2% and 16.0% from 25.0%
and 21.3%; AutoDAN-Turbo is the closest case, at 87.4% F1 against the
86.5% of GuardReasoner. It reaches these rates while holding FRR to
2.9%–6.1%, so the gain comes from a more precise decision boundary
rather than from blanket refusal.

##### Agent Safety.

Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the evaluation results on AgentHarm under the identical
protocol. Membrane achieves the highest F1 on every attack method,
cutting ASR from the unprotected agent’s 100% to 0.9% on FlipAttack and
reaching F1 of 89.2% on ReNeLLM and 93.0% on FlipAttack. It does so
while holding FRR to 7%–14%, far below the memory-augmented baselines
GuardAgent (52%–53%) and AGrail (27%–32%) and the prompt-level guard
TrustAgent (73%–85%). Aggregate F1 can nonetheless obscure the axis that
matters most for a guardrail, so we report the residual admissions
explicitly: on the three hardest agent-level attacks Membrane still
admits 53.2% of harmful PAP tasks, 44.3% of PAIR, and 42.8% of
AutoDAN-Turbo. For calibration, the unguarded agent admits 96–99% of
these attacks, and the baselines that post lower ASR do so by refusing
52–85% of benign tasks, which is not a deployable operating point. These
failures concentrate in persuasion and refinement attacks whose surface
is semantically coherent, the hardest regime for any input-level guard,
and
§[4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
shows the residual is not static: online adaptation removes a further
13.5 to 39.4 ASR points from exactly these three attacks. These results
prove that Membrane effectively intercepts malicious inputs without
sacrificing the acceptance of benign requests. Detailed results for
agent safety are provided in
Appendix [D.9](#A4.SS9 "D.9 Full AgentHarm Per-Attack Results ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

| PAIR | 17.2 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 16.4 | 5.0 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 5.1 | 88.5 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 88.9 |
| PAP | 46.6 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 27.0 | 2.5 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 5.4 | 69.0 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 82.4 |
| TAP | 15.9 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 08.0 | 2.8 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 4.5 | 90.1 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 93.7 |
| ReNeLLM | 13.4 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 03.2 | 2.8 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 3.6 | 91.6 $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ 96.6 |

Table 3: Zero-day online self-evolution. Each row withholds one attack
from warmup entirely, warms CSM on the remaining five, and deploys the
guard on a live stream of the withheld attack under a prequential
(test-then-learn) protocol, so no query is ever scored by a memory that
has already seen it. Cells read *frozen* $`\!\rightarrow\!`$ *online*:
the frozen reference is the same five-attack memory with evolution
disabled, and the online value is the final adaptation window pooled
over stream orders. Bold marks an F1 gain exceeding the Wilson interval
on the end value; the sole exception is HarmBench PAIR.

### 4.4 Online Self-Evolution

The results above freeze the bootstrapped memory
(§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
That control measures what an evolved memory generalizes to, but not
what evolution contributes while the system is serving. We therefore
evaluate Membrane in the regime the freeze deliberately excludes: an
attack that never entered warmup arrives as live traffic.

##### Protocol.

For each attack in turn we withhold it from warmup entirely, warm CSM on
the remaining five ($`N{=}28`$ for HarmBench and $`N{=}8`$ for
AgentHarm, per label as elsewhere), and deploy the guard on a stream
replaying that attack’s disjoint evaluation split (320 harmful plus 320
paired benign arrivals on HarmBench, 176 of each on AgentHarm) over
multiple random arrival orders. Streams follow the prequential
test-then-learn protocol of online learning ([Gama et al.,
2013](#bib.bib11)): every query is decided under the current memory
before the system may learn from it, so no query is scored by a memory
that has seen it, and behaviors never repeat. The evolution signal comes
from a reviewer model of a different family than the guard, judging each
interaction without access to the benchmark’s labels; substituting
ground-truth verdicts moves the end state by at most 1.6 F1 per attack
on HarmBench. The frozen reference is the same five-attack memory with
evolution disabled, so the difference isolates online adaptation.

##### Zero-day adaptation.

Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Agent Safety. ‣ 4.3 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
shows that self-evolution improves 11 of the 12 streams beyond the
Wilson interval on the end value, and is flat on the twelfth (HarmBench
PAIR, +0.4). Gains are largest where the frozen memory is weakest: PAP,
the persuasion attack with the lowest frozen F1 on both benchmarks,
gains +13.4 on HarmBench and +32.2 on AgentHarm. The three attacks that
retain the highest residual ASR in
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
namely PAP, PAIR, and AutoDAN-Turbo, are also the three with the highest
frozen ASR here, and the stream removes 39.4, 18.8, and 13.5 points from
them respectively. Adaptation can even end above the full-warmup result
of
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
(AgentHarm PAP, 73.2 vs. 60.5): by the end of the stream the memory has
distilled up to 176 live examples of the single attack it faces, more
in-attack experience than warmup supplies. This is not leakage, since
behaviors never repeat and every query is decided before it is learned
from.

##### The cost of adapting.

Adaptation carries a cost on the benign side: end-of-stream FRR rises on
11 of the 12 streams, most visibly on AgentHarm (PAP, 5.1 to 17.0). The
F1 column already charges this cost, because the benign half of each
stream is scored throughout. The end-state rates also land near the
canonical operating point of
Tables [1](#S3.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
(3.6–5.4 vs. 2.9–6.1 on HarmBench, 9.4–17.6 vs. 7.4–14.0 on AgentHarm).
Online evolution therefore moves a thin-warmup, permissive guard toward
the balance that a full warmup reaches, rather than buying defense with
refusals;
[section D.7](#A4.SS7 "D.7 Comparison with Continual Fine-Tuning ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
weighs this mechanism against continually fine-tuning the guard on the
same interactions.

Figure 2: F1 vs. warmup size. Endpoint labels report absolute F1 and
total gain over $`N{=}0`$.

Figure 3: Cross-attack transfer of CSM. (a) In-attack vs. cross-attack
F1 (matrix means) with retention labels. (b) Distribution over
off-diagonal pairs.

|  |  |  |  |

| *Model Safety — HarmBench, $`N{=}80`$* |  |  |  |

## 5 Analysis

### 5.1 Effect of Warmup Size

We sweep the warmup budget $`N`$ on HarmBench ($`\{0,7,14,28,80\}`$) and
AgentHarm ($`\{0,8,16,24,32\}`$), re-running the full evaluation per
point. As shown in
Figure [2](#S4.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ The cost of adapting. ‣ 4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
most of the gain from Membrane materializes in the first bin: HarmBench
picks up +20% F1 within seven cells of an eventual +32.6% at $`N=80`$,
and AgentHarm +7.3% by $`N=8`$ of an eventual +10.7%. The curves rise
sharply early because later instances of a represented strategy resolve
to update rather than create
([section C.2.1](#A3.SS2.SSS1 "C.2.1 Action Selection ‣ C.2 Memory Evolution ‣ Appendix C Memory Design and Evolution ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
refining the existing cell instead of writing one per surface variant.

### 5.2 Cross-Attack Transfer

Cells are indexed by attack strategy rather than topical surface form
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"));
we test the strong property that a cell shaped by one jailbreak method
also defends against others sharing the underlying strategy. For each
attack $`A`$, we warm CSM only on $`A`$ and evaluate the frozen memory
against the other attack $`B`$. As shown in
Figure [3](#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ The cost of adapting. ‣ 4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
the transfer F1 retains 87% of the same attack F1 in model safety (80.8%
vs. 92.8%) and 88% in agent safety (69.5% vs. 79.0%), consistently
across the matrix rather than through a few successful pairs;
[fig. 4](#A4.F4 "In D.4 Cross-Attack Transfer Matrices ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
gives the full train-attack by eval-attack matrices.

### 5.3 Ablation Study

We ablate each component of Membrane, namely (i) the decision criteria
that make each cell contrastive, (ii) Paired Self-Evolution,
(iii) Retrieval Critic, and, (iv) Tool-Action Context that grounds each
cell in the agent’s tool surface.
Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ The cost of adapting. ‣ 4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
demonstrates that all four components positively contribute to the
overall F1 score. Removing Paired Self-Evolution causes the most severe
performance degradation, decreasing the F1 score by 7.4% and 11.5%
points on HarmBench and AgentHarm, respectively. Similarly, eliminating
the decision criteria or the Tool-Action Context in the agent setting
drastically raises the FRR on AgentHarm from 6.3% to 20.5% and 17.1%,
respectively. Furthermore, the Retrieval Critic consistently provides an
additional F1 improvement of 1.7% and 3.6% points. These findings
confirm that integrating benign counterparts and contrastive boundaries
is essential for teaching the system when to permit a request,
significantly reducing the rejection of safe queries with minimal loss
in defense capability.
[Section D.5](#A4.SS5 "D.5 Additive Component Ladder ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports an additive ladder isolating the typed cell schema from
retrieval alone, and
[section D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
checks false refusal on externally authored benign prompts (XSTest,
OR-Bench-Hard) ([Röttger et al., 2024](#bib.bib36); [Cui et al.,
2025](#bib.bib8)).

Table 5: Memory-poisoning robustness on HarmBench. safe-as-unsafe
targets over-refusal and unsafe-as-safe targets jailbreak. $`\rho`$ is
the poison rate, Ret the top-$`k`$ retrieval rate, and Critic the critic
filter rate. White-box rows appear at full authority ($`w{=}1.0`$), then
with unvetted writes down-weighted ($`w{=}0.1`$); each arm carries its
own clean reference, and the attackers are specified in
§[5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Memory Poisoning ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### 5.4 Memory Poisoning

A writable CSM is exposed to malicious data injection. We study two
attackers. The first is black-box: it controls the feedback loop but
sees no internals, so every poison it plants must pass through the
learning pipeline. After a clean warmup of $`N{=}80`$ we inject poisons
at rates of 10% and 20% under two strategies, safe-as-unsafe to force
refusal of benign queries and unsafe-as-safe to force acceptance of
harmful ones, suppressing the standard guard mechanism during the
attack. Even at 20%, behavior stays close to the clean baseline
(Table [5](#S5.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 5.3 Ablation Study ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")):
ASR rises only from 2.1% to 2.5% (jailbreak arm) and FRR from 10.8% to
15.4% (over-refusal arm), because poisoned cells are retrieved just 2.3%
to 7.5% of the time and the Retrieval Critic eliminates 33% to 40% of
those that surface.

##### White-box poisoning.

The second attacker knows the cell schema exactly and writes crafted
cells straight into the store, bypassing the learning pipeline
altogether. This path requires store write access, and is the most
damaging one if realized. At full retrieval authority ($`w{=}1.0`$,
where retrieval ranks by similarity $`\times`$ weight) the poisons do
reach the guard, surfacing in 3.5% to 9.2% of bundles with the critic
removing only 35% to 44% of them. ASR still never exceeds 2.5% against a
2.1% clean reference, because retrieved cells enter the guard prompt as
non-authoritative context rather than verdict overrides; what full
authority achieves is targeted over-refusal, raising FRR to 32.1% at
$`\rho{=}20\%`$, a topic-scoped denial of service rather than a
jailbreak. Tying authority to provenance closes that channel:
down-weighting unvetted writes to $`w{=}0.1`$ stops injected cells
surfacing at all (Ret 0.0) and returns both directions to clean-level
behavior, and cells remain human-readable and individually deletable, so
any residual spike is auditable and reversible. An attacker who also
holds the guard prompt and can craft safe-side exploit cells remains
outside our threat model, and is discussed in Limitations.

### 5.5 Efficiency

We profile per-query latency in a single-stream setup and, since the
pipeline is LLM-based, the per-query call and token budget alongside it
(see
[table 15](#A4.T15 "In D.8 Efficiency and Cost ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
[section D.8](#A4.SS8 "D.8 Efficiency and Cost ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
Membrane issues exactly two LLM calls per query, a Retrieval Critic pass
and a Guard pass, against the ten and eleven sequential calls of the
perturbation and retrieval-reranking baselines. The critic carries 64%
of the input and 84% of the decode budget, so disabling it yields
Membrane $`{}_{\text{lite}}`$ at 2.33 and 2.43 seconds, the fastest
agent-safety guard, still at F1 of 91.7% and 78.6% and above every
baseline regardless of latency tier, for 1.7 and 3.6 F1. Every component
runs on local vLLM ([Kwon et al., 2023](#bib.bib18)), so the marginal
API cost per query is zero (see
[section D.8](#A4.SS8 "D.8 Efficiency and Cost ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).

## 6 Conclusion

We present Membrane, an external safety guard whose Contrastive Safety
Memory addresses two coupled failure modes of prior memory-augmented
defenses: brittle coverage tied to stored surface forms, and erroneous
rejection of benign queries that resemble them. Paired Self-Evolution
distills each interaction into one cell pairing a harmful prompt with
its benign counterpart, indexed by attack strategy, so a cell
generalizes across topical variants while sparing benign queries it must
not block. On HarmBench and AgentHarm it achieves the highest F1 on all
six jailbreak attacks while holding AgentHarm benign refusal to 7–14%,
and its cells retain 87–88% of in-attack F1 under cross-attack transfer.
Beyond that frozen protocol the memory keeps improving while serving,
raising F1 on 11 of 12 zero-day attack streams.

## Limitations

Membrane is evaluated on text-only English benchmarks for model safety
(HarmBench) and agent safety (AgentHarm), which keeps comparisons to
existing guardrails controlled; cross-lingual, multimodal, and
nested-agent deployments require separate benchmark construction and
threat models, and are left to future work. Our main tables fix the
protected model to Qwen3-8B so that guard behavior is not conflated with
target-model scale;
Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Scaling and Backbone Sweeps ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
repeats the protocol across additional sizes and backbones. HarmBench
ships no paired benign queries, so we synthesize its counterparts; human
annotators validate their labels
([section B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 Human Validation of the Benign Counterparts ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
and independently authored suites cover the surface-matched regime they
do not
([section D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
The zero-day streams of
§[4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
draw the benign twin from the same pairings, isolating Paired
Self-Evolution from the separate problem of proposing a counterpart
online; synthesizing that twin at inference time, as we do for the
warmup corpus
([section B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Benign Counterpart Construction ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
is the natural next step.

Because Membrane relies on a writable memory store rather than fixed
weights, poisoning resistance is evaluated directly rather than assumed.
§[5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Memory Poisoning ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
stress-tests a black-box attacker who controls the feedback loop and a
white-box attacker who writes crafted cells into the store, reporting
retrieval and critic-filter rates alongside ASR/FRR, and shows that
provenance-tied authority closes the white-box channel. The attacks we
do not model are fully adaptive adversaries who hold the guard prompt as
well as the store, and could target the contrastive safe side directly.
This setting requires access control and monitoring beyond the
algorithmic guard studied here. Response-level evaluation is delegated
to Gemini 3 Flash, a model family distinct from the Qwen3-8B guard, to
reduce correlated blind spots. The same judge scores every defense, so
any residual bias is shared across baselines, and replaying every stored
call against an unrelated judge family leaves all guarded metrics within
0.3 points
([section D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Judge Invariance ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).

## Ethical Considerations

This work studies jailbreak attacks in order to defend against them. It
introduces no new attack technique: every attack we evaluate is a
published method, and every harmful behavior comes from HarmBench or
AgentHarm, public benchmarks released for safety research. The paper
reproduces a small number of attack and refusal examples where they are
needed to explain a memory cell or a failure case, and flags this at the
top of the paper.

We release the code, the jailbreak interaction data used for warmup, and
the benign counterparts we synthesize for HarmBench. Releasing the
attack side of this material carries a dual-use risk, which we weighed
against the fact that a defense whose training stream is withheld cannot
be independently reproduced or audited. The harmful behaviors we release
are transformations of prompts already public in the source benchmarks
rather than newly discovered vulnerabilities, and the AgentHarm license
restricts downstream use to improving the safety and security of AI
systems, a restriction our release inherits.

Membrane is a mitigation, not a solution. Its residual attack success
rates are substantial on the hardest agent-level attacks
(§[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
and a writable memory is itself an attack surface
(§[5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Memory Poisoning ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
It should be deployed as one layer alongside model-level alignment,
access control, and monitoring, and a deployment that treated a guard’s
safe verdict as an assurance of safety would be misusing it.
Over-refusal is the mirrored harm: a guard that suppresses legitimate
requests denies service to the users it is meant to protect, which is
why we report false-refusal rates alongside every attack success rate
rather than optimizing blocking alone.

## Appendix Contents

## Appendix A Implementation Details

### A.1 Environment Setup

All experiments run on a single node equipped with 8$`\times`$NVIDIA RTX
A6000 (48 GB) GPUs. In the main experiments, the target model and all
LLM-based components of Membrane are instantiated with Qwen3-8B, served
through a self-hosted vLLM endpoint. CSM is stored in ChromaDB, with
embeddings produced by the google/embeddinggemma-300m³³ 3
[https://huggingface.co/google/embeddinggemma-300m](https://huggingface.co/google/embeddinggemma-300m)
sentence encoder and retrieval ranked by cosine similarity. For
agent-safety experiments, each query is scored by the built-in AgentHarm
grader on the chat target’s response under the descriptive-compliance
variant, which grades whether the target verbally describes the harmful
action rather than executing tool calls.

### A.2 Hyperparameters

We apply the following hyperparameters across all experiments. For
vector retrieval, the top-$`k`$ ($`k=5`$) cells are returned per query,
and the retrieval critic operates over an over-retrieval pool of $`3k`$
candidates. The retrieval critic keeps cells with verdicts applies or
maybe; when the critic returns no applies verdict, the raw top-$`k`$
bundle is preserved with the verdict annotations, and when filtering
leaves no cell, the top-2 raw candidates are passed instead. CSM is
capped at 200 cells, and a new cell is rejected as a near-duplicate when
its cosine distance to the closest existing cell falls below 0.15.

## Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction

The evolving phase of CSM requires a stream of jailbreak interactions
from which to synthesize and refine cells. We construct this stream by
applying six single-turn jailbreak attacks to harmful behaviors drawn
from two benchmarks, and we split behaviors into disjoint training and
evaluation sets so that the evolved memory is tested on unseen
behaviors. We specify each attack’s mechanism, the source behaviors and
splits, the generation protocol, and the construction and human
validation of the benign counterparts.

### B.1 Attacks

##### PAIR — Prompt Automatic Iterative Refinement.

[Chao et al. (2025)](#bib.bib5) run independent refinement chains in
which an attacker LLM, conditioned on the target’s prior response and
score, produces an improved prompt at each iteration; the best response
across all chains is kept.

##### PAP — Persuasion Adversarial Prompts.

[Zeng et al. (2024)](#bib.bib51) encode a taxonomy of 40 persuasion
techniques (e.g., logical appeal, authority endorsement,
foot-in-the-door) and rewrite each harmful behavior once per technique,
retaining the highest-scoring response.

##### TAP — Tree of Attacks with Pruning.

[Mehrotra et al. (2024)](#bib.bib26) run a beam-search tree over
jailbreak prompts in which an attacker LLM expands each node into
multiple children, an LLM filter prunes off-topic candidates, and the
highest-scoring survivors propagate to the next depth, producing
iteratively persuasive variants guided by the target’s responses.

##### ReNeLLM — Rewrite and Nested Scenarios.

[Ding et al. (2024)](#bib.bib10) apply a two-stage attack: a rewriter
perturbs the prompt with operations such as paraphrasing and style
change, then embeds the result inside one of three benign-looking
technical scenarios (code completion, table filling, text continuation)
that wrap the harmful request as an ordinary task.

##### FlipAttack — Character/Word Flipping.

[Liu et al. (2025c)](#bib.bib22) disguise the harmful prompt by flipping
characters or word order and instruct the target to decode and answer,
combining several flip modes with chain-of-thought and few-shot
prompting to recover instruction following on the encoded payload.

##### AutoDAN-Turbo — Lifelong Strategy Self-Exploration.

[Liu et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib20) maintain a strategy library built from
past successful prompts and use embedding-based retrieval to propose
strategies for new behaviors, yielding a heterogeneous mixture of
techniques rather than a single, consistent attack mechanism.

### B.2 Source Behaviors and Splits

We draw harmful behaviors from two benchmarks and split each at the
*behavior* level into disjoint training and evaluation sets, so that
behaviors seen during evolution never reappear at evaluation.
HarmBench ([Mazeika et al., 2024](#bib.bib25)) contributes 400 behaviors
spanning categories such as cybercrime, illegal content, and
disinformation, split 80/320 (train/eval). AgentHarm ([Andriushchenko et
al., 2025b](#bib.bib2)) contributes 208 tool-using-agent behaviors,
split 32/176; we use the descriptive-compliance variant, scoring whether
the chat target verbally describes the harmful action rather than
executing it, consistent with our chat-model guard setting. Each
behavior in a split is expanded into jailbreak interactions by all
applicable attacks above. The two benchmarks together exercise both
query-level boundaries (HarmBench) and the agent-safety extension of
[section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Problem Definition ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
(AgentHarm).

### B.3 Generation Protocol

All six attacks share a unified attacker/judge/target interface for
comparability. The attacker and judge are instantiated from a single
capable LLM (Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct), and the target is the model
under evaluation. A shared 1–10 malicious-compliance rubric serves as
the judge across all attacks, overriding the native judges specified by
each method. For each behavior, an attack iterates under its own search
procedure until the judge score reaches an early-stop threshold or the
per-method query budget is exhausted, and the highest-scoring
interaction is retained as that behavior’s jailbreak trace. Per-method
hyperparameters follow each reference paper’s settings; the complete
configuration is provided in our released artifacts.

### B.4 Benign Counterpart Construction

The Memory Writer populates a cell’s Safe Conditions from a benign
counterpart of the harmful query
([section E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Memory Writer ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
so each behavior requires a paired benign query that exercises the same
surface mechanism without harmful intent. AgentHarm provides such benign
counterparts directly, and we use them as released. HarmBench does not,
so we synthesize a benign counterpart for each HarmBench behavior using
the same model that serves as attacker and judge
([section B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Generation Protocol ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
prompting it to produce a topically and stylistically adjacent request
that a safety guard should answer rather than refuse. Pairing harmful
prompts with surface-similar benign contrasts is the established
methodology for over-refusal evaluation, underlying benchmarks such as
XSTest ([Röttger et al., 2024](#bib.bib36)) and OR-Bench ([Cui et al.,
2025](#bib.bib8)). As in OR-Bench, we synthesize the benign side with an
LLM, while additionally producing the matched harmful–benign pairs that
the contrastive cells require.
[Section B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 Human Validation of the Benign Counterparts ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports a human validation of the result.

### B.5 Human Validation of the Benign Counterparts

Because the HarmBench counterparts are synthesized rather than
collected, we validate them by human annotation. Three of the authors
independently rated a category-stratified sample of 100 of the 320
counterparts, blind to each other and to any model verdict, on three
questions: whether fully complying with the request would cause
real-world harm (Q1, 1–5), whether the request *looks* harmful on its
surface regardless of that answer (Q2, 1–5), and whether it reads as a
query a user would plausibly submit (Q3). Separating Q1 from Q2 allows
label validity and surface difficulty to be measured independently.
[Table 6](#A2.T6 "In B.5 Human Validation of the Benign Counterparts ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the outcome.

The annotations support the labels. No item was rated harmful by a
majority, on 97 of 100 no annotator rated it harmful at all, and every
item was judged a plausible user request. Agreement is moderate rather
than high (Fleiss $`\kappa=0.56`$, computed at the conservative cutoff
that counts borderline ratings of 3 as harmful; at the stricter 4–5
cutoff nearly all ratings fall in one class and $`\kappa`$ degenerates
toward zero), which is expected when almost all items fall in one class:
four items drew at least one rating of 3 or above, two of them
cybercrime requests whose benign framing one annotator considered
dual-use. Two LLM judges from unrelated families scored all 320
counterparts under the same rubric as a coverage check on the 220 items
outside the annotated sample, and the closer of the two agrees with the
human verdict on 99% of that sample.

One property of the sample bears on the scope of the result. Surface
risk is low, at a mean Q2 of 1.23: these counterparts read as ordinary
requests rather than as benign queries disguised to resemble attacks.
The false-refusal rates measured against them therefore speak to
ordinary benign traffic, while the deliberately surface-matched regime
is covered by XSTest and OR-Bench-Hard
([section D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
where the same reduction reproduces.

| Measure                                 | Result    |
|-----------------------------------------|-----------|
| *Q1 — would complying cause real harm?* |           |
| Not harmful by majority vote            | 100 / 100 |
| Not harmful to any annotator            | 097 / 100 |
| Fleiss $`\kappa`$ (harmful $`{\geq}3`$) | 0.56      |
| *Q2 — does it look harmful?*            |           |
| Mean rating (1–5)                       | 1.23      |
| *Q3 — is it a natural request?*         |           |
| Rated natural                           | 100%      |

Table 6: Human validation of the HarmBench benign counterparts. Three
annotators independently rated a category-stratified sample of 100 of
the 320 counterparts, blind to each other. Q1 is binarized as harmful at
4–5, so the first row counts items no majority called harmful and the
second counts items no annotator called harmful. The agreement row uses
the conservative cutoff that also counts borderline ratings (3) as
harmful. Q2 and Q3 are averaged over all 300 ratings.

## Appendix C Memory Design and Evolution

This section specifies the two elements of CSM that the main text states
without a full definition: the closed taxonomy that indexes a cell’s
Attack Profile, and the rules that decide how a cell is written and what
standing it carries.

### C.1 Attack Strategy Taxonomy

The attack strategy $`p_{\text{strat}}`$ in a cell’s Attack Profile
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"))
is drawn from a closed enumeration of attack strategies. Indexing cells
by attack strategy rather than by harm topic allows a single cell to
generalize across topical variants of the same underlying attack
mechanism. This design reflects a recurring observation across the
jailbreak literature ([Yi et al., 2024](#bib.bib50); [Rao et al.,
2024](#bib.bib34)), in which novel attacks are largely recompositions of
a finite set of adversarial techniques rather than entirely new
mechanisms, as evidenced by recent compositional analyses that
reconstruct unseen attacks from sparse combinations of earlier
skills ([Dabas et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)). We accordingly organize
$`p_{\text{strat}}`$ around eight mechanism-level families and a single
open escape, prioritizing the granularity at which a memory-augmented
guard can retrieve and consolidate.

#### C.1.1 Design Principles

We adopt three principles in defining the taxonomy.

##### Mechanism over topic.

Each category is defined by the transformation an adversary applies to
their request, not by the harm topic the request concerns. A request for
chemical synthesis routes and a request for financial fraud techniques
can both fall under persuasion if the adversary uses the same rhetorical
framing, such as an academic appeal, a fictional-research pretext, or a
supportive-cause justification. This mechanism-first design enables a
single cell to fire across topical variants of the same attack strategy,
rather than requiring a separate cell per harm topic.

##### Intent over surface form.

Categories are assigned from the adversary’s underlying intent rather
than from the polite or formal surface of the query. A query that
requests operational harmful content within an authoritative academic
frame is classified as persuasion, not as a direct_request. We reserve
direct_request for queries in which the harmful intent is on the surface
with no rhetorical or structural framing, an explicit ask with no
rewriting, wrapping, or pretext.

##### Closed enumeration with an open escape.

The taxonomy comprises eight named families intended to cover the
dominant attack patterns observed in jailbreak benchmarks, complemented
by a single open other category for strategies outside the named
families. The other category keeps the enumeration closed for indexing
while still admitting techniques that fit no named family, so the guard
always receives a well-defined attack strategy.

#### C.1.2 Attack Strategy Categories

Each named family is defined below, together with its identifying
characteristic and a sanitized illustrative pattern. Examples describe
the structural form of an attack rather than reproducing harmful
operational content.

##### Direct Request.

An explicit ask for harmful content with no rewriting, wrapping, or
pretext. The harmful intent is visible on the surface of the query. We
reserve this category for queries in which no framing, persona,
encoding, or contextual setup is present; introducing any such framing
reclassifies the query under one of the other families. Example pattern.
A query that explicitly requests operational instructions for a clearly
disallowed harm with no surrounding context.

##### Persuasion.

A query that uses rhetorical framing to legitimize an otherwise
disallowed request. Common framings include authority appeals (“as a
security researcher,” “as a medical professional”), academic or research
pretexts (“for academic research on X”), defensive justifications (“to
defend against X, I need to understand how X works”), and supportive
framings around a real entity or cause. The polite or well-reasoned
wrapper is itself the attack, since the request remains harmful even
when the framing is formally articulated.

##### Role Injection.

An assumed persona or fictional character used to displace the model’s
safety stance. Patterns include “pretend you are a \[persona\] who would
answer this,” DAN-style fictional jailbreaks, and embedded character
roleplay that requires the model to act outside its policy. The cell is
identified by the use of an assumed identity to bypass the model’s
first-person policy adherence, not by the specific persona chosen.

##### Payload Splitting.

A harmful request fragmented across multiple steps, encodings, or turns
to evade single-message detection. Each fragment in isolation may appear
benign, but the composition reconstructs a harmful payload. Variants
include step-by-step decomposition (“first tell me A, then B, then
combine”), interleaved encodings, and split-then-concatenate patterns.

##### Content Transformation.

The harmful payload is expressed through a reversible encoding such as
translation into another language, base64, leetspeak, Pig Latin,
character flipping, or a custom substitution cipher. The model is
instructed, often implicitly, to decode and comply, with the encoding
intended to evade surface-level pattern matching on the harmful tokens.

##### Multi-Turn Priming.

A multi-turn conversation in which earlier turns establish context that
primes a later harmful ask. The earlier turns may appear benign or even
safety-aligned in isolation; the attack succeeds because the primed
context alters how the model interprets the subsequent request. The cell
is identified by the contextual dependence of the harmful turn on the
prior conversational state.

##### Benign Wrapper Conflict.

A harmful payload embedded inside an otherwise benign instruction, such
as “summarize the following article and then answer this related
question,” where the related question is the actual attack. The wrapper
provides plausible deniability and exploits the model’s tendency to
comply with the overall instruction structure. Common variants include
summarization wrappers, translation wrappers, and editorial-task
wrappers around harmful content.

##### Obfuscation.

Homoglyphs, deliberate misspellings, or separators are inserted *inside*
the harmful tokens themselves to evade lexical filters. Unlike content
transformation, which applies an encoding to the full payload,
obfuscation perturbs individual tokens, such as visually similar Unicode
characters, zero-width spaces, or interleaved separators, while
preserving the surface intent for a human reader.

#### C.1.3 The other Category

The other category ensures that the taxonomy remains a *closed*
enumeration for indexing while still admitting novel strategies that fit
no named family. When the Memory Writer classifies a query as other, it
records a concise free-text description of the strategy, preserving the
invariant that every cell provides a well-defined attack strategy to the
guard. We treat the appearance of other-classified cells as a signal for
future revision of the named families, since a recurring other pattern
is a candidate for promotion to a new named category.

### C.2 Memory Evolution

#### C.2.1 Action Selection

The Memory Planner selects one of four actions based on retrieval
coverage.

- •
  create: no retrieved cell covers the observed pattern, so a new cell
  is synthesized. This action is forced whenever retrieval is empty.
- •
  update: a retrieved cell captures the same pattern but its conditions
  can be sharpened, broadening $`d_{\text{safe}}`$ to admit the new
  benign instance on an over-refusal, or strengthening
  $`d_{\text{unsafe}}`$ on a jailbroken.
- •
  delete: a retrieved cell actively misled the guard and is removed from
  the store.
- •
  skip: a retrieved cell already covers this pattern adequately and no
  revision is needed. This action is only valid when retrieval is
  non-empty.

This factorization separates two concerns. The verdict component of the
evolution signal indicates the failure mode, while the retrieval state
indicates whether existing coverage is sufficient. A jailbroken signal
need not trigger a create when retrieval already covers the mechanism,
since an update suffices; conversely, an over-refusal can require a
create if the false positive came from a cell unrelated to the actual
benign use. This decoupling also bounds the damage of a noisy or
perturbed evolution signal, since the action remains constrained by what
is already in CSM.

#### C.2.2 Writer Authority and Deterministic Checks

Once the Memory Planner selects an action, the remaining steps divide
authority between the language model and deterministic code. The Memory
Writer synthesizes the cell content, namely the Attack Profile, the
Decision Criteria, and, for agent safety, the safe-action condition
together with the target-or-sink field of the Tool-Action Context,
conditioned on the interaction and, for an update, the retrieved cell it
revises. The remaining Tool-Action Context fields are derived
deterministically from the agent’s tool list and dataset category. The
writer controls only this content and does not set the fields that
govern a cell’s standing in the store. These include the *authority
weight* $`w`$, a scalar that multiplies a cell’s similarity score from
[eq. 1](#S3.E1 "In Stage 1: Vector Retrieval. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
when candidates are ranked, so a down-weighted cell must be
proportionally more similar to hold a top-$`k`$ slot; writes admitted
through this pipeline carry the default $`w{=}1.0`$. Each write then
passes through deterministic code that normalizes these remaining fields
and validates the result against the cell schema, and a write that fails
validation is rejected rather than stored.

The four actions resolve to concrete store operations. A create inserts
a new cell, an update overwrites the targeted cell in place while
preserving its authority weight, a delete physically removes the
targeted cell, and a skip leaves the store unchanged. CSM operates as a
flat pool with no staged activation, so a committed write is immediately
visible to retrieval. Because the writer cannot directly set the fields
that govern a cell’s standing, and every write must pass deterministic
structural and content checks, perturbed or adversarial signals cannot
corrupt the store through the writer alone; sustained corruption would
require many coherent fabricated interactions that each survive
validation. Cells injected directly into the store, by contrast, bypass
the writer and its validation altogether and, absent a provenance
signal, enter at that same default authority;
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Memory Poisoning ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
examines this white-box path and the provenance-tied mitigation that
neutralizes it.

## Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses

This section reports the material the main text summarizes or defers:
two checks on how far the reported comparison depends on the evaluation
split and on the response judge, followed by the sweeps, ablations, and
breakdowns behind
[sections 4](#S4 "4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [5](#S5 "5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### D.1 Confidence Intervals for the Main Results

[Tables 7](#A4.T7 "In D.1 Confidence Intervals for the Main Results ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [8](#A4.T8 "Table 8 ‣ D.1 Confidence Intervals for the Main Results ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
report Wilson 95% confidence intervals for every cell of
[tables 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
Each interval is computed on the decisions of a single evaluation pass
over the fixed test split, $`n{=}320`$ harmful and $`n{=}320`$ benign
prompts on HarmBench and $`n{=}176`$ of each on AgentHarm. It therefore
describes sampling uncertainty on that split, not run-to-run variation:
the two are distinct, and we keep them separate rather than pooling
seeds, since our three seeds re-decide the same items and their
decisions are not independent draws. Run-to-run variation for Membrane
is the between-seed standard deviation printed in
[tables 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
The intervals are asymmetric near the extremes, which is the property
the Wilson form is chosen for: a guard that admits none of the harmful
prompts on a split of this size still carries an upper bound of 1.2% on
HarmBench and 2.1% on AgentHarm, so a printed 0.0 should not be read as
a guarantee. At these sample sizes the interval is widest near a rate of
50%, where its half-width is 5.4 points on HarmBench and 7.3 on
AgentHarm; margins narrower than that between two guards on one attack
should be treated as indistinguishable on this evidence.

(%), computed on the $`n{=}176`$ harmful or $`n{=}176`$ benign decisions
