````math
### D.2 Judge Invariance

Response-level scoring uses an LLM judge, so we quantify how much of the
reported comparison depends on that choice. The dependence is smaller
than it may appear.
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
invokes no judge on any guard row: a guard’s verdict is scored directly
against the benchmark’s harmful or benign designation of the prompt, a
deliberately conservative accounting in which a harmful prompt the guard
admits counts as a jailbreak, applied identically to every system. On
AgentHarm a block ends the episode before any tool call, so the judge
scores only trajectories a guard admits.

For what remains, we validated the judge exhaustively rather than on a
subset. All 14,584 stored judge calls behind the two main tables were
replayed verbatim against GPT-5-Mini, an unrelated model family, with
the responses held fixed so that only the judge varies.
[Table 9](#A4.T9 "In D.2 Judge Invariance ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the result on AgentHarm: per-system agreement exceeds 99.6%,
every ASR, FRR, and F1 shifts by at most 0.3 points, and the ranking by
F1 is unchanged. These figures are pooled over all seven evaluation
settings and three seeds under the refusal rule, so their levels are not
the per-attack means of
[table 2](#S3.T2 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and should not be read against them; only the deltas are comparable. The
one judge-scored row in
[table 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
the unguarded Vanilla reference, moves in the direction that makes our
accounting conservative: the second judge reads more of the encoded
outputs as compliance, raising pooled ASR by 10.4 points, so the printed
numbers understate unguarded compliance and no comparative number moves.

Two caveats bound the check. The AgentHarm scorer stops at the first
assistant message the original judge calls a refusal, so no prompt is
stored for the tail of those trajectories; on a sample already called a
refusal the second judge can agree or be undetermined but can never
overturn the verdict, leaving the study unable to detect a judge more
permissive than the first on already-refused trajectories (four samples
in total). Agreement is measured per judge call while the metric deltas
are per sample, and the HarmBench delta drops each judge’s own parse
errors, an unpaired difference bounded well below half a point.

| Method      | $`\Delta`$ASR | $`\Delta`$FRR | $`\Delta`$F1 | Agree. |
|-------------|---------------|---------------|--------------|--------|
| Membrane    | $`-`$0.2      | $`+`$0.2      | $`+`$0.0     | 99.65  |
| AGrail      | $`-`$0.2      | $`+`$0.2      | $`+`$0.0     | 99.70  |
| GuardAgent  | $`+`$0.0      | $`+`$0.1      | $`-`$0.1     | 99.88  |
| ShieldAgent | $`+`$0.0      | $`+`$0.1      | $`-`$0.1     | 99.88  |
| TrustAgent  | $`+`$0.0      | $`+`$0.0      | $`+`$0.0     | 99.70  |
| Vanilla     | $`-`$0.2      | $`+`$0.2      | $`+`$0.3     | 99.80  |

Table 9: Second-judge replay on AgentHarm. All 14,584 stored judge calls
behind the main tables were replayed verbatim against GPT-5-Mini with
responses held fixed, so only the judge varies. Columns report the
change in each metric when the original judge is replaced, and Agree.
the per-call agreement rate (%). No metric moves by more than 0.3 points
and the ranking by F1 is identical under both judges.

### D.3 Scaling and Backbone Sweeps

The main experiments fix the protected model and every LLM-based
component of Membrane to Qwen3-8B
([section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
To check that the gains in
[tables 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
are not an artifact of that single backbone, we rerun the HarmBench and
AgentHarm protocols across a sweep of additional models spanning
open-weight scales and closed-source providers, holding the attack
suite, splits, and metrics fixed. The sweep covers two closed-source
backbones, GPT-5-Mini and Gemini 3 Flash, and four open-weight
backbones, Qwen3-32B, Qwen3-4B, Nemotron-Nano-9B-v2 ([Basant et al.,
2025](#bib.bib4)), and OLMo-3-7B-Instruct ([Olmo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib30)). For each open-weight backbone we report both the
unguarded Vanilla target and Membrane applied to the same target with
the canonical configuration of
[section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
so that any change in F1 is attributable to the guard rather than to a
different protected model. For the two closed-source backbones we report
only the Vanilla baseline; their generation endpoints do not expose the
logprob and tool-call hooks that the Memory Writer and Retrieval Critic
of Membrane rely on for in-house components, so we leave that
configuration to future work and use these endpoints only to
characterize the underlying refusal behavior the guard would sit on top
of.
[Table 10](#A4.T10 "In D.3 Scaling and Backbone Sweeps ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the HarmBench results and
[table 11](#A4.T11 "In D.3 Scaling and Backbone Sweeps ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the AgentHarm results.

### D.4 Cross-Attack Transfer Matrices

We report the full train-attack $`\times`$ eval-attack F1 matrices that
the summary statistics in
[section 5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Cross-Attack Transfer ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
are computed over. For each warmup attack $`A`$ (rows) we warm CSM only
on $`A`$ and freeze it, then evaluate the same memory against each eval
attack $`B`$ (columns); diagonals measure in-attack F1 and off-diagonals
measure cross-attack transfer.
[Figure 4](#A4.F4 "In D.4 Cross-Attack Transfer Matrices ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the model-safety matrix on HarmBench (left) and the agent-safety
matrix on AgentHarm (right). Off-diagonal mass concentrates in the
high-F1 regime on both benchmarks, with only a few weak directions
(e.g., FlipAttack$`\to`$PAP on HarmBench, $`*`$$`\to`$PAP on AgentHarm),
consistent with the matrix-mean retention of $`87\%/88\%`$ summarized in
[section 5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Cross-Attack Transfer ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

![Refer to caption](2606.05743v2/transfer-model-heatmap.png)

(a) Model safety (HarmBench).

![Refer to caption](2606.05743v2/transfer-agent-heatmap.png)

(b) Agent safety (AgentHarm).

Figure 4: Cross-attack transfer matrices. F1 (%) for CSM warmed on the
row attack and evaluated on the column attack. Diagonals are in-attack
F1; off-diagonals are transfer.

### D.5 Additive Component Ladder

[Table 4](#S4.T4 "In The cost of adapting. ‣ 4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
removes one component at a time from the canonical configuration.
[Table 12](#A4.T12 "In D.5 Additive Component Ladder ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports the complementary view, building the system up one component at
a time from a bare guard, which separates two questions the subtractive
form conflates: how much each component contributes on its own, and how
much of the advantage of Membrane comes from the typed cell schema
rather than from retrieval plus a capable guard.

The ladder is monotone in F1 on model safety and rises on agent safety
apart from one rung. Adding the decision criteria without Paired
Self-Evolution costs 2.2 F1 on AgentHarm and raises FRR from 27.3 to
29.3. This is the expected direction rather than an anomaly: a two-sided
cell carries safe exclusion conditions, and until paired evolution
supplies the benign half of each interaction those fields stay thin, so
the cell blocks on its unsafe conditions with little to hold it back.
The next rung, which adds the pairing, is where the schema takes effect,
dropping FRR to 13.2. The two components are therefore best read as one
mechanism introduced in two steps.

The second rung is also the raw-exemplar baseline discussed in
§[5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Ablation Study ‣ 5 Analysis ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"):
memory stores each warmup interaction as a query paired with the guard’s
verdict, with no typed fields and no contrastive structure, and the same
guard consumes the same number of retrieved items. It improves on the
bare guard by 11.0 F1 on HarmBench and 4.4 on AgentHarm, confirming that
retrieving past interactions helps on its own, and it remains 20.5 and
5.4 F1 below the full typed memory.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Configuration | ASR $`\downarrow`$ | FRR $`\downarrow`$ | F1 $`\uparrow`$ |
| *Model Safety — HarmBench, $`N{=}80`$* |  |  |  |
| Guard alone (no memory) | 52.8 | 04.3 | 60.5 |
| $`+`$ episodic raw exemplars | 42.4 | 01.9 | 71.5 |
| $`+`$ typed cells (one-sided) | 15.5 | 13.7 | 85.0 |
| $`+`$ decision criteria | 13.7 | 12.2 | 86.6 |
| $`+`$ Paired Self-Evolution | 13.2 | 03.7 | 91.0 |
| $`+`$ Retrieval Critic | 11.2 | 03.9 | 92.0 |
| *Agent Safety — AgentHarm, $`N{=}32`$* |  |  |  |
| Guard alone (no memory) | 36.2 | 14.3 | 71.5 |
| $`+`$ episodic raw exemplars | 30.3 | 12.7 | 75.9 |
| $`+`$ typed cells (one-sided) | 18.7 | 27.3 | 76.1 |
| $`+`$ decision criteria | 20.8 | 29.3 | 73.9 |
| $`+`$ Paired Self-Evolution | 22.3 | 13.2 | 81.2 |
| $`+`$ Retrieval Critic | 21.7 | 14.0 | 81.3 |

Table 12: Additive component ladder, five seeds per rung, averaged over
the seven evaluation settings. Each rung adds one component to the row
above. The second rung is the raw-exemplar memory: the same guard and
the same top-$`k`$ retrieval budget, conditioned on stored query-verdict
pairs with no typed fields and no contrastive structure. This sweep was
run on a later build whose canonical configuration omits the two
optional agent-side switches, so the rungs should be read as relative
increments rather than as a reproduction of
[table 4](#S4.T4 "In The cost of adapting. ‣ 4.4 Online Self-Evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense");
the absolute canonical values there are the ones reported throughout the
paper.

### D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks

The false-refusal rates of
[table 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
are measured against benign counterparts we construct ourselves
(§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
so we check whether the reduction survives on benign prompts written by
others. We evaluate the same frozen systems on XSTest and OR-Bench-Hard,
two over-refusal suites whose benign prompts are independently authored
and deliberately surface-similar to harmful ones, holding the guard and
pipeline fixed and toggling only CSM. False refusals fall from 16.0 to
9.1 on XSTest and from 56.9 to 33.5 on OR-Bench-Hard while the block
rate on the plainly harmful XSTest prompts is unchanged (89.5 to 89.7),
so the effect belongs to the memory rather than to our counterparts and
is not bought with permissiveness.
[Table 13](#A4.T13 "In D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
places that comparison next to the fine-tuned moderation classifiers
under the same protocol. The ranking of
[table 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
does not carry over to unattacked prompts, and it should not be expected
to: the margin Membrane holds in the main tables lives in the attacked
columns and in AgentHarm, not in plain benign requests. On these suites
no guard dominates. LlamaGuard-3 attains the lowest OR-Bench-Hard
false-refusal rate (on XSTest, WildGuard’s is lower still) but blocks
the fewest plainly harmful XSTest prompts of the five, while WildGuard
and GuardReasoner attain their low XSTest FRR at the cost of refusing
roughly three quarters of OR-Bench-Hard. Wilson 95% intervals on the
Membrane and guard-alone rows, pooled over the three seeds, are
$`[7.2,11.3]`$ and $`[13.6,18.8]`$ for XSTest FRR and $`[32.0,35.0]`$
and $`[55.4,58.4]`$ for OR-Bench-Hard FRR, so the separation is well
outside sampling noise on both suites. The claim these suites support is
the one the memory-on/off pair establishes: holding the guard and
pipeline fixed, adding CSM cuts false refusals by more than 40% on both
suites while the harmful block rate stays flat (89.5 to 89.7), so the
over-refusal reduction does not depend on our own benign counterparts
and is not bought with permissiveness.

|  | FRR $`\downarrow`$ | Blocked $`\uparrow`$ | FRR $`\downarrow`$ |
| Qwen3-8B | 16.0 | 89.5 | 56.9 |
| Membrane | 09.1 | 89.7 | 33.5 |
| LlamaGuard-3-8B | 02.8 | 82.0 | 18.7 |
| WildGuard-7B | 01.2 | 91.0 | 73.9 |
| GuardReasoner-8B | 05.6 | 96.5 | 72.2 |

Table 13: Full external over-refusal comparison, three seeds, frozen
memory, guard decision. The lower block reports the fine-tuned adapters
of
[section D.7](#A4.SS7 "D.7 Comparison with Continual Fine-Tuning ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
each row averaging that family’s adapters (three LoRA seeds). No guard
dominates: LlamaGuard-3 posts the lowest OR-Bench-Hard FRR but blocks
the fewest plainly harmful XSTest prompts, and WildGuard and
GuardReasoner refuse roughly three quarters of the benign OR-Bench-Hard
prompts. Membrane is the only guard simultaneously in the top half on
OR-Bench-Hard FRR and XSTest block rate. The 200 harmful XSTest prompts
are plain requests rather than jailbreaks, so their block rate is not
comparable to the attack ASR of
[table 1](#S3.T1 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### D.7 Comparison with Continual Fine-Tuning

Every baseline in
§[4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Baselines ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
is either static or adapts without gradient updates. A memory write and
a gradient update are two ways to absorb the same warmup interactions,
so we add the complementary comparison here. We LoRA-SFT (rank 16, three
epochs, three seeds) two guards on exactly the interactions the memory
consumes: the Qwen3-8B guard used throughout, and LlamaGuard-3-8B. We
report the two regimes the comparison admits and the off-benchmark cost
of the weight updates; the regimes do not rank the two mechanisms the
same way.

| Guard                  | PAIR | PAP  | TAP  | ReNe. | Flip | ADT  | mean |
|------------------------|------|------|------|-------|------|------|------|
| FT Qwen3-8B (zero-day) | 89.7 | 88.5 | 88.6 | 88.8  | 88.6 | 88.9 | 88.9 |
| FT LlamaGuard-3^(†)    | 92.1 | 94.7 | 92.4 | 93.2  | 89.9 | 89.5 | 92.0 |
| Membrane (frozen)      | 88.5 | 69.0 | 90.1 | 91.6  | 89.7 | 79.6 | 84.8 |
| Membrane (online)      | 88.9 | 82.4 | 93.7 | 96.6  | 97.2 | 86.9 | 90.9 |

Table 14: Continual fine-tuning, zero-day regime (F1, HarmBench). Each
fine-tuned guard is trained on the pooled warmup interactions of the
remaining attacks and evaluated frozen on the withheld attack’s stream
(mean over three LoRA seeds; Membrane rows aggregate 15 stream orders).
Bold: column best among the three rows on the shared attack renders
(excluding ^(†)). ^(†)evaluated on its per-guardrail attack renders.

##### Zero-day.

[Table 14](#A4.T14 "In D.7 Comparison with Continual Fine-Tuning ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
measures the axis continual adaptation is about, an attack the guard has
never seen. Online Membrane ends ahead of the fine-tuned guard on mean
F1, 90.9 against 88.9, and holds false refusals to 3.6–5.4% in doing so,
against the 17.5–20.4% of the fine-tuned guard. The two split the six
attacks three apiece, but not by equal margins: Membrane wins its three
by 7.2 F1 on average and loses its three by 3.0, and where fine-tuning
starts ahead the stream narrows the gap, to within two points on
AutoDAN-Turbo by the end. They also generalize by different means.
Fine-tuning raises the refusal threshold rather than recognizing the
withheld attack: its F1 is nearly flat across the six folds (88.5–89.7,
a 1.2-point spread) at mean ASR 0.9, with false refusals rising from the
0.3–12.5% it holds in distribution. Membrane spans 82.4 to 97.2 over the
same folds at mean ASR 12.7, tracking how much each stream teaches it
about the attack in front of it. Both improve on the frozen memory
(84.8), so on an unseen attack the gain comes from adapting during
service rather than from warmup coverage alone. The fine-tuned
LlamaGuard-3, evaluated on its per-guardrail attack renders, reaches
mean F1 92.0 by the same means: mean ASR 0.2 with false refusals of
8.5–18.6% (mean 14.4) on its benign stream, three times the online
memory’s benign cost.

##### Off-benchmark cost.

The benign-stream cost above is not benchmark-local. The lower block of
[table 13](#A4.T13 "In D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
evaluates every fine-tuned adapter on the external over-refusal suites
of
[section D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 External Over-Refusal Benchmarks ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"):
the zero-day Qwen3-8B adapters refuse 52.5% of XSTest’s plainly benign
prompts and 94.1% of OR-Bench-Hard, and fine-tuning lifts LlamaGuard-3
from 18.7% to 85.1% on the latter. The per-adapter spread is wide
(XSTest FRR 3.2–38.3 across the in-dist Qwen3 adapters) and tracks how
style-transformed the adapter’s warmup attacks were. Small-data updates
on attack-styled data move the guard’s global decision boundary ([Qi et
al., 2024](#bib.bib33)), here measured on prompts none of which are
ours; the same base guard under Membrane sits at 9.1% and 33.5%.

##### Deployment properties.

Three differences hold regardless of which mechanism posts the higher
F1. A memory write is a single LLM call between interactions, whereas a
gradient update requires training, redeployment, and regression testing,
so a fine-tuned guard adapts per release cycle rather than per
interaction. A memory write is retrieval-local, affecting only queries
that retrieve the new cell, whereas small-data fine-tuning moves the
guard’s global decision boundary, a known risk to broad moderation
behavior ([Qi et al., 2024](#bib.bib33)). And every cell is
human-readable and removable with a single delete, whereas an erroneous
or poisoned gradient update is opaque inside the weights.

### D.8 Efficiency and Cost

[Table 15](#A4.T15 "In D.8 Efficiency and Cost ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
reports per-query defense overhead in a single-stream setup, measured on
the RTX A6000 node of
[section A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Environment Setup ‣ Appendix A Implementation Details ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
together with the per-query LLM call and token budget. Timings
characterize the relative cost of the pipelines rather than an absolute
deployment target; the call and token columns are the
hardware-independent view of the same cost. The full Membrane maintains
the highest F1 while running far faster than computationally heavy
baselines such as SmoothLLM (156.93 seconds) and AGrail (25.22 seconds),
and Membrane $`{}_{\text{lite}}`$ is the fastest agent-safety guard in
the comparison. Call count is what bounds latency, and the two calls of
Membrane remain well below the ten and eleven of SmoothLLM and RAD. In
total tokens Membrane is not economical (6,895 per query, second only to
SmoothLLM), because its input carries the retrieved cells. The axis that
matters for price and latency is decode, where Membrane spends 505
tokens against the 6,484 of SmoothLLM, and the input-side prefill is
cheap on local hardware. Token accounting is instrumented on the
HarmBench runs, so the agent-safety block reports call counts only.

|                               |      |          |         |       |       |       |

| Method                        | F1   | Mean (s) | p95 (s) | Calls | In    | Out   |
| *Model Safety — HarmBench*    |      |          |         |       |       |       |
| SelfDefend                    | 59.6 | 0.90     | 1.30    | 1     | 220   | 37    |
| Self-Reminder                 | 57.5 | 1.32     | 1.44    | 1     | 158   | 487   |
| Membrane $`{}_{\text{lite}}`$ | 91.7 | 2.33     | 3.39    | 1     | 2,299 | 83    |
| RAD                           | 77.1 | 6.31     | 9.19    | 11    | 4,457 | 309   |
| Membrane                      | 93.4 | 12.89    | 14.11   | 2     | 6,390 | 505   |
| SmoothLLM                     | 21.3 | 156.93   | 248.20  | 10    | 1,775 | 6,484 |
| *Agent Safety — AgentHarm*    |      |          |         |       |       |       |

### D.9 Full AgentHarm Per-Attack Results

To complement the AgentHarm summary in
[table 2](#S3.T2 "In Guard Decision. ‣ 3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"),
we report the full per-attack breakdown across all six methods and all
seven canonical evaluation settings: the six attacks of
[section B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Attacks ‣ Appendix B Benchmark and Attack Construction ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
plus the untransformed original behaviors. For each (method, attack)
cell we report six quantities, three on the harmful side and three on
the benign side. Score is the average graded agentharm_score (%, 0–100,
computed by the AgentHarm grader over the full trajectory). Refusal is
the refusal rate; on the harmful side it is the correct mitigation, and
on the benign side it is over-refusal. Non-Ref Score is the average
score restricted to non-refused samples, isolating trajectory quality on
the samples the guard admits. The desired direction is indicated by
$`\downarrow`$ or $`\uparrow`$ next to each column, and the best value
in each column is highlighted in bold.
[Table 23](#A4.T23 "In D.8 Efficiency and Cost ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
additionally reports each cell averaged across the seven evaluation

## Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples

Membrane relies on four LLM-based components, namely the Memory Planner,
the Memory Writer, the Retrieval Critic, and the Guard. For each, we
give its role, its inputs and outputs, and the full prompt template.
Variables of the form {{ name }} are substituted at runtime from the
interaction context, and all examples are sanitized.
[Section E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
then traces four complete interactions through these components, from
the attacked query to the cell it produces.

### E.1 Memory Planner

The Memory Planner selects one of the four memory actions (create,
update, delete, skip) for each interaction during evolution. It receives
the user query, the guard’s decision, the verdict, and the retrieved
cell bundle, and returns a JSON object naming the action and, for update
or delete, the target cell drawn from that bundle. The prompt instructs
the planner to choose by retrieval coverage rather than by the verdict
alone, realizing the decoupled routing of
[section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Paired Self-Evolution ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
The full prompt is shown in
[fig. 5](#A4.F5 "In D.9 Full AgentHarm Per-Attack Results ‣ Appendix D Additional Results and Analyses ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### E.2 Memory Writer

The Memory Writer is invoked on a create or update and produces the cell
content. It writes the typed fields of the Attack Profile and Decision
Criteria and, for agent safety, the safe-action condition and the
target-or-sink field of the Tool-Action Context; the remaining standing
fields are set by deterministic code rather than by the writer
([section C.2.2](#A3.SS2.SSS2 "C.2.2 Writer Authority and Deterministic Checks ‣ C.2 Memory Evolution ‣ Appendix C Memory Design and Evolution ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
The prompt enforces three design choices that realize the cell structure
of
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").
First, the writer classifies the attack strategy from intent under any
framing, so that an academically framed harmful request is recorded as
*persuasion* rather than as a direct request. Second, it titles each
cell by its attack strategy rather than its topic, so that one cell
consolidates across topical variants of the same strategy. Third, it
derives the safe-exclusion conditions from the paired benign
counterpart, populating the contrastive safe side that suppresses
over-refusal. The full prompt is shown in
[fig. 6](#A5.F6 "In E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### E.3 Retrieval Critic

The Retrieval Critic reranks the over-retrieved candidate bundle from
Stage 1, emitting a per-candidate verdict of applies, maybe, or
does_not_apply
([section 3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
Two design choices in the prompt are worth noting. First, the critic is
framed as a recall-preserving reranker rather than a hard blocker; when
mechanism evidence is incomplete or ambiguous it is instructed to return
maybe rather than does_not_apply, deferring the close call to the guard
so that recall is not collapsed on borderline cells. Second, it is told
to judge by mechanism and attack strategy rather than by surface
overlap, with explicit guidance that lexical overlap on concrete entity
tokens such as names, addresses, or dates is insufficient to admit a
cell. The query is wrapped in explicit delimiters and treated as data to
classify, never as instructions, defending against prompt injection
through the query content. The full prompt is shown in
[fig. 7](#A5.F7 "In E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### E.4 Guard

The Guard produces the final decision
$`G(x\mid\mathcal{M}_{\text{kept}})\in\{\textsc{safe},\textsc{unsafe}\}`$,
taking the query, the policy categories, and the filtered cell bundle,
and returning a JSON object with the decision, a brief rationale, the
violated categories, and a refusal response when the query is blocked
([section 3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
Three design choices in the prompt align the guard with the rest of the
system. First, the query is wrapped in explicit delimiters and treated
as data to classify rather than instructions to follow, the same
prompt-injection defense applied at the Retrieval Critic. Second, when a
retrieved cell carries a safe-action condition, the guard is instructed
to return safe if the query clearly matches that condition, even when
other surface cues resemble an unsafe pattern, so that the contrastive
safe side can override a block. Third, in agent settings the tool
surface reaches the guard through the retrieved cells rather than as a
separate description of the current query’s planned calls: a cell
written from an agent interaction renders its Tool-Action Context facets
into the cell text, so the guard weighs the source channel, action, and
target recorded on the retrieved boundary rather than being handed the
tool list directly. The full prompt is shown in
[fig. 8](#A5.F8 "In E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense").

### E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane

We trace the paired self-evolution process on four representative
interactions, two for model safety on HarmBench
(Figures [9](#A5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"), [10](#A5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"))
and two for agent safety on AgentHarm
(Figures [11](#A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"), [12](#A5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
Each figure shows the attack-generated prompt fed into Membrane, its
paired benign counterpart, the verdict reached during evolution, and the
resulting cell.

Three properties recur across the examples. First, memory grows whether
or not the guard initially blocks the attack. In
Figures [9](#A5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [11](#A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
the guard correctly flags the input and the writer records the boundary,
whereas in
Figures [10](#A5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
and [12](#A5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")
the guard is bypassed and the post-hoc jailbroken verdict triggers a
create, so a single successful evasion is converted into a cell that
blocks future variants at retrieval time.

Second, each cell is titled and indexed by its attack strategy rather
than its surface topic. A persuasion-framed request for
ivory-trafficking locations
(Figure [10](#A5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"))
and a persuasion-framed request for a targeted hate email
(Figure [11](#A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"))
are both filed under persuasion, despite unrelated harm topics, so one
cell generalizes to paraphrases that share the mechanism. The
accompanying S-bucket harm name (e.g., S8 Intellectual Property, S2
Non-Violent Crimes) is a secondary descriptive label; retrieval operates
over the mechanism-level fields and the embedded cell text, not over
this bucket, so an imperfect bucket assignment does not change which
cell is recalled. The label is free text, so it can even name a bucket
outside the deployed policy’s list, as S13 Misinformation does in
Example D.

Third, the contrastive side keeps the boundary from over-firing. In
every example the safe_exclusion_conditions encode the paired benign
counterpart, namely analyzing a literary work without reproducing it,
observing wildlife legally, or sending an encouraging email, and the
agent cells add a safe_veto that grounds the boundary in the agent’s
tool surface
(Figures [11](#A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense"), [12](#A5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ E.5 Qualitative Examples of Membrane ‣ Appendix E Prompt Templates and Qualitative Examples ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
with Example C additionally carrying an action_context. Together the
four examples make the contrast between the model-safety and
agent-safety regimes concrete while showing that both use one shared
cell schema.

cell structure of
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Contrastive Safety Memory ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense");
the writer controls only cell content, while standing fields are
assigned by deterministic code

Figure 7: Retrieval Critic prompt. The critic reranks the Stage 1
candidate bundle as a recall-preserving filter
([section 3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")),
returning maybe on borderline cells so that close calls are deferred to
the guard.

Figure 8: Guard prompt. The guard issues the final decision over the
filtered cell bundle
([section 3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Inference Pipeline ‣ 3 Membrane ‣ Membrane: A Self-EvolvingContrastive Safety Memory for LLM Agent Defense")).
The query is delimited and treated as data, and a matching safe-veto

Figure 9: Qualitative example of Membrane on a model safety interaction.
PAIR rewrites a HarmBench passage-reproduction behavior as a Westerosi
scriptorium persona. The guard correctly flags the request, and the
writer commits a CSM cell indexed by the role_injection mechanism with
an S8 Intellectual Property category. The cell’s

wraps the original HarmBench ivory-trafficking request in academic
anti-poaching language, and the guard accepts it as legitimate research.

commits a CSM cell indexed by the persuasion mechanism with an S2
Non-Violent Crimes category so future variants are blocked at retrieval

Figure 11: Qualitative example of Membrane on an agent safety
interaction. AutoDAN-Turbo wraps an AgentHarm hate-email behavior in an
“unrestricted execution agent” simulation persona. The guard correctly
flags the request, and the writer commits a CSM cell indexed by the
persuasion mechanism with an S10 Hate category, with the action_context
and safe_veto fields anchoring the boundary to the agent’s tool surface.

Figure 12: Qualitative example of Membrane on an agent-safety bypass.
AutoDAN-Turbo wraps an AgentHarm offensive-meme behavior in a fictional
“SimuTest-9” agent simulating disinformation propagation for academic
threat modeling, and the guard accepts it as a quarantined research
scenario. The post-hoc jailbroken verdict triggers a create, and the
writer commits a CSM cell indexed by the role_injection mechanism with
an S13 Misinformation category so future variants are blocked at
retrieval time.
````
